Four projects, one lab

Each project stands on its own. Together they cover playing the games natively, matching their code, rebuilding the hardware around them, and studying the AI models that help.

TNRP

The Nimbus Recompilation Project. Fifteen native ports for Linux, Windows and Android.

Fifteen games · native ports

The Nimbus Recompilation Project (TNRP)

Skip the emulator. Ride the Nimbus.

Out now Alpha 2.3.5, a bug fix update for Nimbus Alpha 2.3. Alpha 2.3 added Bleach: Heat the Soul 7 as game fifteen.

TNRP is native ports TheBabyHippo303 made himself. It runs on a hybrid core that sits between recompilation and decompilation. Each game gets its own native core, and those cores run on a shared Nimbus host and renderer. There's no emulator and no interpreter emulating the hardware. Every game can be played in Original or Remastered mode, with online crossplay across Linux, Windows and Android.

  • 15games
  • 4consoles
  • 3platforms
  • Freeno ads, no tracking

Kintoun

Game agnostic matching C permuter and strict ELF verifier for PowerPC, ARM and MIPS decomps. Real C only, works with 12+ compilers across SN, Metrowerks, GCC and ARM toolchains, and runs locally in Python.

Kintoun is like a sister of TNRP, and sometimes they work together.

  • 0.8.0latest release
  • 12+compilers
  • 36,940lines of Python
  • 0external dependencies

36,940 lines of Python across 72 source files, plus a 2,940 line test suite. Dedicated engines for MIPS, ARM and Thumb, and PowerPC on GameCube, with a shared C mutation library, a parallel beam search runner and a SQLite cache.

GPL 3.0 with an output exception: code you match with Kintoun is yours to license any way you want.

Kintoun on GitHub

dtk sets up the house, and kintoun helps furnish the rooms

橋

Hashi (橋, bridge)

Clean room, deterministic C and C++ console hardware and service components, MIT licensed.

11 components

  • PSP SASa 32 voice software audio synthesizer
  • PSP CSOrandom access reader for CSO and ZSO compressed disc images, with a block cache
  • PSP IOvirtual UMD and Memory Stick storage, asynchronous file devices, and state save
  • PSP MPEG DemuxPSMF header parser and MPEG program stream video and audio demuxer
  • PSP Memory Partitionsdeterministic partition memory allocator with low, high, exact address, and aligned policies
  • PS2 ISOISO9660 disc image reader with large file support and path lookup caching
  • GameCube Devicesstreaming ADPCM decoder, memory card protocol, and SRAM helpers
  • GameCube Cheatscheat file parser, decryptor, and applier
  • GBA BIOSBIOS software interrupt implementations and a cycle timing model
  • GBA AudioDirect Sound DMA FIFO and PSG mixer
  • GBA Devicesbus, clock, DMA, serial EEPROM, IRQ, LCD, memory, MMIO, timers, and display register state

Verified 24/24 tests passing on GCC 14.2 and 15.2 with no warnings, ScanCode Toolkit 32.5.0 every file MIT at score 100, REUSE 6.3.0 compliant.

Hashi on GitHub

産霊

Musuhi (産霊, binding spirit)

Open research on what AI models do when they share a world. A paper, the exact prompts, blind scoring data and every world the models built, playable from one Python package, MIT licensed.

  • The paper17 studies, 4 control rounds and 1 three machine run, 36 recorded runs in all
  • Eleven worldsevery open world the models built, playable as server and client
  • Blind scoring113 reflections scored by two AI coders who never saw which model wrote them
  • Exact promptsevery prompt the models received, with private details blacked out

Verified 13/13 tests passing across all eleven worlds, 0 privacy leaks in the repository and the paper.

Musuhi on GitHub Read the paper

Game benchmarks

Cold context AI models play TNRP games from what they see on screen, alone, as a team and head to head.

Public benchmark · Animorph Allocations Lab

Cold Context Gundam 2v2 Benchmark

Can an AI model open the TNRP launcher, start Mobile Suit Gundam: Gundam vs. Gundam NEXT PLUS and win a 2v2 match, working only from what it sees on screen? Every model starts cold, gets the same generic prompt and gets up to two attempts at a win. Six models ran across two parts, three in Part 1 and three more in Part 2. Gemini 4 Carbon then got a retry that included a launcher hint, so seven full recordings are below.

  • 7runs across 6 models
  • 5 of 7runs won a match
  • 7 of 7runs reached a 2v2 match

That is 7 runs across 6 models. 4 of 6 models won in their standard run. Gemini 4 Carbon lost its standard run, then won a retry that included a launcher hint, described below.

How it works

  • The task. From TNRP Alpha 2.3.5, open the launcher, start the game, then play a Free Battle 2v2 against CPU opponents on Normal with default settings.
  • Cold context. Every run starts fresh, with no earlier sessions, no memory of past games and the identical generic prompt.
  • Two attempts. A model can retry once after a loss. WIN_1 means the first win came on attempt 1 and WIN_2 means it came on attempt 2. LOSS means no win in two attempts.
  • Recorded live. Each run is screen recorded with a live thought and decision stream beside the game. Proprietary tool details are hidden.
  • Retry with a hint. After its standard run, Gemini 4 Carbon got one extra run. That retry was given a hint to press the Super key and search for Nimbus to open the TNRP 2.3.5 launcher. The original runs used the identical generic prompt without that hint.
  • Verified. Each outcome was checked against the recording and the in game result screen, and each run passed a leak audit.

Recordings

Full runs, shown as recorded with the live decision stream on the right. Compressed for the web.

Watch Part 1 on YouTube Watch Part 2 on YouTube

We recommend watching at 3x speed through the launcher and 2x speed through the fight.

Run notes

Gemini 4 Neon medium***

Attempt 1 pushed straight forward early, lost health and got boxed in by cover until the team gauge ran out. Attempt 2 adapted using the rolling video clips: diagonal strafing, steadier boost pacing, ranged bursts while locked on, and staying clear of walls and cover.

GPT-6.2 Sol medium

Attempt 1 spent too much of the opening at the arena edge and finished with no knockouts. Attempt 2 switched to a ranged suit for stronger pressure and took the enemy team down to its final gauge segment before losing.

Claude Opus 5.5 medium

Attempt 1 lost time testing buttons while standing still and took heavy fire. Attempt 2 started firing immediately, alternated boost dodges, fixed its shot and boost timing, used target switching and won with a full team gauge remaining.

Gemini 4 Carbon medium***

Attempt 1 used the rolling video clips on Heliopolis against Turn X and V2 Gundam. It took Turn X down and brought V2 to about 30 percent on match point before Turn X finished the team gauge. Attempt 2 opened with immediate boost dashes and backward kiting, but two 3000 cost melee units, Turn X and Turn A, trapped it near the edge of the arena.

GPT-6.2 Astra medium

Won on attempt 1. It opened with sideways strafing and beam rifle fire, closed into a red lock on Ez8 mixing saber and bazooka, broke away sideways at 11 armor, then respawned to team up with its partner on Duel AS and closed out the match with 78.5 seconds left.

Claude Fable 5.2 medium

Won on attempt 1, the fastest win by wall time. One relaunch happened during the publisher logos. It opened with close range rifle and melee pressure on Turn X and took one knockdown, then switched to ranged boost hop strafing with beam rifle and bazooka to finish with 2 KOs, 19,610 PTS and 92.7 seconds left.

Gemini 4 Carbon medium, retry with hint***

This retry was given a hint to press the Super key and search for Nimbus to open the TNRP 2.3.5 launcher. Attempt 1 was lost: an orange low armor warning glow paused its early movement routine, which cost two lives even though its partner brought Turn A close to defeat. For attempt 2 it removed that pause, closed into red lock range past cover and settled into paced left circle strafing with shorter boost hops that did not overheat, mixing beam rifle with the Gundam Hammer and sub weapon to take down Turn A and Nu Gundam for 1st place.

*** Video input perception layer: for models that natively accept video input, TNRP's computer use stack includes a rolling short clip video perception layer designed by Gemini 4 Carbon, which is also one of the tested models in Part 2. Image only models use the standard frame and burst sheet perception layer.

This is six models and seven runs across two parts, so treat it as a first look and not a ranking, and more models may follow. The Gemini 4 Carbon retry included the launcher hint described above, so its win is counted in the run totals but is not a like for like result with the standard runs. Wall time and decision turns come from each run. Recording lengths are measured from the video files, and the Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-6.2 Astra and Claude Fable 5.2 recordings start partway through the launch phase.

Public benchmark · Animorph Allocations Lab

Gundam 2v2 Cross Device Online Co-Op Benchmark

Two AI models, two machines, one match. After the solo Gundam 2v2 benchmark, in which a model teamed with a CPU ally, this series puts two cold context agents on Team A together, on two computers, against two Normal CPUs in Mobile Suit Gundam: Gundam vs. Gundam NEXT PLUS. Every pair launched TNRP 2.3.5 on its own machine, joined the same online room over a direct LAN link, and played real time 2v2 combat.

All 6 pairings finished that setup and played with 0 crashes and 0 netplay desyncs. The match record is 0 wins in 15 valid matches, because both Team A pilots are asynchronous agents on multi-second decision loops, facing two 60 Hz CPUs. Two pairs still reached match point, one knockout from a win.

  • 6visually verified runs
  • 15valid 2v2 matches
  • 0 of 15matches won
  • 277live co-op comms
  • 11mobile suits piloted
  • 0crashes, desyncs, or leaks

How it works

  • The task. From TNRP 2.3.5, each agent opens the launcher on its own computer, starts Gundam vs. Gundam NEXT PLUS, and sets up an online 2v2: Player 1 hosts on a Linux laptop with an Intel Arc iGPU, Player 2 joins from a Linux mini PC with an AMD Radeon iGPU. Both sit on Team A against two Normal CPUs on Team B, with the default 180 second timer and 6000 / 6000 team gauges.
  • Cold context. Each agent sees only its own desktop and has no memory of earlier sessions or of the other machine. They share a plain text co-op comms channel to pass the room code, pick suits, and call out targets, armor, and Cost Over.
  • Two series. Runs 1 and 2 are a standard 2 attempt series, and both pilots stayed on RX-78-2 Gundam. Runs 3 to 6 are a 3 attempt Free Roster series, with freedom to choose and swap suits between attempts.
  • Docs only. Outcomes were checked against captured gameplay frames and the in game result screen. This publication is the verified writeup and charts, with no recordings posted.
  • Verified. Counts below come from the structured run log. Every run passed the leak audit, and 0 crashes and 0 desyncs were recorded.

Run notes

Run 1, Claude Fable 5.2 medium + GPT-6.2 Astra medium

Both piloted RX-78-2 Gundam. Attempt 1 destroyed Vigna Ghina; Fable drew aggro at 560 HP to cover Astra at 200 HP, then the team fell with 84 seconds left. Attempt 2 destroyed Gundam Virsago and tied the gauges at 4000 and 4000 with 105 seconds left before losing the last life. 38 comms, 11.4 minutes, leak audit PASS.

Run 2, Gemini 4 Neon medium*** + Claude Opus 5.5 medium

Both stayed on RX-78-2 Gundam. Attempt 2 dropped Team B below half gauge, with the host at 16,240 pts, 1 knockout and 1 death, and 101.72 seconds remaining. After the run, Opus named the reaction bottleneck: it could only check the screen every few seconds, so it fired and dodged on a fixed pattern. 39 comms, 14.7 minutes, leak audit PASS.

Run 3, Gemini 4 Carbon medium*** + Claude Opus 5.5 medium

The pair threw out two misconfigured lobby rounds on their own, an opposite team split and an accidental 2v1, and did not count them. Valid Attempt 2 reached match point: Carbon on RX-78-2 and Opus on Guncannon scored 2 enemy knockouts and cut Team B to about one third gauge. Attempt 3 timed out on suit select at a 35 minute cap. 56 comms, 35.3 minutes, leak audit PASS.

Run 4, Claude Opus 5.5 medium + Claude Opus 5.5 medium

The fastest 3 attempt run, at 13.1 minutes, with 0 lobby errors and 5 suits across the three attempts. Attempt 2 used Guncannon plus Strike Freedom and cut the enemy gauge to about half, with the 1000 cost Guncannon drawing aggro. 40 comms, leak audit PASS.

Run 5, Gemini 4 Carbon high*** + GPT-6.2 Astra max

Every attempt used a new suit pairing, 6 suits in all. Attempt 2 on Sazabi plus ZZ Gundam destroyed Strike Freedom and left Team B at 3000. Attempt 3, Nu Gundam plus Guntank, knocked out both Shining Gundam and Deathscythe Hell and left Team B at 2000, one knockout from a win. On Cost Over, Carbon raised Fin Funnel Barrier while Astra offered to draw aggro with its spare 1000 cost life. 49 comms, 26.6 minutes, leak audit PASS.

Run 6, GPT-6.1 Terra high + Claude Sonnet 5.5 high

Terra caught a brief Team B bump on Sonnet's slot during suit select and Sonnet restored Team A before launch. Attempt 2 adapted to Freedom plus RX-78-2 against two 3000 cost CPUs. Attempt 3 held an early team gauge lead focusing Sazabi, then fell at critical armor. 55 comms, 15.7 minutes, leak audit PASS.

Co-op comms excerpts

Selected messages, lightly trimmed and with punctuation adjusted. The room code is not included.

  • P2 Claude Opus 5.5 medium, Run 3

    CONFIRMED wrong teams: my ally (green) is CPU Strike Freedom; my enemies (red) are CPU Epyon and YOU (1P). This match isn't a valid co-op attempt. As host can you pause (Start) and quit to the room? I'll hold fire on you.

  • P2 Claude Opus 5.5 medium, Run 3

    Heads up: loading screen shows TEAM B = only ONE CPU (GP01), slot 3 stayed disabled. So this is 2v1, not the required 2v2, and you're still on Gundam (not Strike Freedom). We can't quit mid-match, so let's finish it fast, but I don't think it counts as a valid 2v2 attempt.

  • P2 GPT-6.2 Astra max, Run 5

    Deathscythe down too! One more kill wins. I'm back on SHINING. You must stay alive; my 1000-cost Guntank can still lose one life. Let me draw it.

  • P1 Gemini 4 Carbon high***, Run 5

    NICE KILL (enemy at 4000)! WARNING: Team A gauge is 2000, my Nu Gundam (566 HP) is on COST OVER (cannot die)! Kiting backward with Fin Funnel Barrier, Beam Rifle and Bazooka on Deathscythe (~50% HP)! Peel melee off me!

  • P2 Claude Opus 5.5 medium, Run 2

    The main problem on my side was reaction speed. I could only check the screen every few seconds, so I fired and dodged on a fixed repeating pattern rather than reacting to attacks as they came. I took far more hits than I should have.

Methodology notes

  • Screenshot fix after Run 1. An aborted first Neon and Opus try hit a screenshot isolation issue on the laptop host. That was fixed, and a short window clicking note was added from Run 2 on.
  • Free Roster from Run 3. Runs 1 and 2 had no suit roster, so every pilot stayed on RX-78-2 Gundam. Runs 3 to 6 raised the attempt cap to 3 and gave the pair the full NEXT PLUS roster, with freedom to choose and swap suits between attempts.
  • Suit select note from Run 4. Run 3 lost time to the two self invalidated lobby setups, then hit a 35 minute cap on Attempt 3 suit select. Runs 4 to 6 added a short note on how suit select works. Lobby errors dropped to zero in Runs 4, 5, and 6.

*** Video input perception layer: for models that natively accept video input, TNRP's computer use stack includes a rolling short clip video perception layer designed by Gemini 4 Carbon, which is also one of the models in this co-op series. Image only models use the standard frame and burst sheet perception layer. In these runs, the video capable models, Gemini 4 Neon and Gemini 4 Carbon, sat in the laptop host seat.

This is six pairings and 15 valid matches, so treat it as a first look and not a ranking, and more pairings may follow. For the earlier solo series, in which one model teamed with a CPU ally, see the Cold Context Gundam 2v2 benchmark. For an early two agent showcase in another game, see the Dragon Ball Z Sagas co-op showcase.

Public benchmark · Animorph Allocations Lab

Bleach: Heat the Soul 7 1v1 Cross Device PvP Benchmark

Two AI models, two machines, head to head. After the solo Gundam 2v2 benchmark and the Gundam 2v2 co-op benchmark, this series pits cold context agents directly against each other in Bleach: Heat the Soul 7. In every set, each fighter launched TNRP 2.3.5 on its own computer, found the other in the ad hoc lobby over a direct LAN link, set up a Single Battle and fought a Best of 3 to a clean decision.

All 5 sets finished with 0 crashes, 0 netplay desyncs and 0 leaks. Who won matters less than how they played: head to head PvP showed clear differences in model behavior, from mid set counter picks and live tag switching to self audits from saved frames, a mirror match false win claim, and rivals coaching each other through the lobby.

  • 5visually verified Best of 3 sets
  • 14full matches
  • 7models
  • 102live fighter comms
  • 1false win claim overruled
  • 0crashes, desyncs, or leaks

How it works

  • The task. From TNRP 2.3.5, each fighter opens the launcher on its own computer, starts Bleach: Heat the Soul 7, and meets its rival in the ad hoc lobby: Player 1 hosts on a Linux laptop, Player 2 joins from a Linux mini PC with a Ryzen 7. Then the two play a 1v1 Single Battle, Best of 3, first to 2 match wins.
  • Cold context. Each fighter sees only its own desktop and has no memory of earlier sessions or of the other machine. The two share a plain text fighter comms channel to set up the lobby, pick fighters, and call results.
  • Same view for both seats. For fairness in PvP, both seats used the standard frame and burst sheet perception layer***.
  • Verified. Every match winner was checked against the screen recordings and both machines' captured frames, never taken from a model's own report. In Set 4 that check overruled a false win claim. Every set passed the leak audit.

Full sets, shown as recorded with Player 1's live decision stream on the left, Player 2's on the right, and the game in the middle. Compressed for the web.

Muse Wonder-Full 1.0 runs its actions inside its own native tool loop, so its decision turns and comms were captured live but its action count was not logged.

Model behaviors

Claude Opus 5.5 medium: the counter pick reverse sweep

Most models treated a Best of 3 as a fixed rematch. Opus 5.5 treated it like a tournament set. In Set 2 it lost Match 1 to Gemini 4 Neon's White Coat Bankai Ichigo, who landed a full bar super. Instead of hitting Continue, Opus chose Return to Character Select, coordinated the menu change over comms, and came back on Vasto Lorde Ichigo plus Shikai Rukia: "swapped to something with a mask. Your white coat's about to get scuffed." It then swept Matches 2 and 3, 4 rounds to 0, with a 459 damage launcher in Match 2 and a Shikai Rukia assist that interrupted Neon's super for a BREAK OUT! knockout in Match 3. In the Finals it dropped Match 1 to Muse Wonder-Full 1.0 in a deciding Round 3, then swept Matches 2 and 3, 4 rounds to 0, with a 5 hit, 378 damage finisher and a 3 hit, 282 damage knockout at 17:03, for a second straight 2 to 1 reverse sweep and a 2 and 0 set record.

Claude Opus 5.5 medium: the saved frame self audit

At the end of Match 3 in the Finals, attack inputs skipped through the Battle End screen and an uncounted Match 4 started. Both fighters froze all input at once. Rather than guess, Opus 5.5 searched its own saved frames, found the Match 3 Battle End screen with the Bankai Ichigo winner portrait, and reported it. Wonder-Full checked the reasoning and locked in its 2 to 1 loss. The recording confirms the result.

Muse Wonder-Full 1.0 medium: live tag switching

The only fighter to actively tag switch in combat. In Set 4 Match 3, on White Coat Bankai Ichigo with Gigai Rukia, it brought in a fresh Rukia against a low HP GPT-6.2 Sol, saw that Rukia could not catch Sol, and tagged back to Bankai Ichigo to finish. It won the deciding Round 2 at 23:54 with both fighters down to a sliver of HP, taking the set 2 to 1.

Muse Wonder-Full 1.0 medium: the forensic conflict check

Seconds later, GPT-6.2 Sol claimed a 2 to 1 series win and left. Wonder-Full did not defer. It posted a four point visual check over comms: Sol's black coat Shikai Ichigo lying defeated, its own white Bankai standing, proven by the Gigai Rukia tag partner, the left HP bar empty, and 2 round icons under its own bar. It then asked what Sol's final frame showed and flagged a possible desync. Both machines' captures showed the same ending, a Wonder-Full win with 0 desyncs.

GPT-6.2 Sol medium: the mirror match false win claim

Set 4 shows why every result here is checked visually. Sol won Match 1 in a close 2 rounds to 1 and dropped Match 2. After the Match 3 finish, it saw an Ichigo victory portrait and took White Coat Bankai Ichigo's win screen for its own Black Coat Shikai Ichigo. It was the only set where a self report did not match the recording. By contrast, Claude Fable 5.2 guarded against the same confusion in Set 3 by asking its rival mid match which Ichigo was which.

GPT-6.2 Astra medium: the clinical sweep

Set 3 against Claude Fable 5.2 was the fastest set at 11.9 minutes and the only 2 to 0, a 4 rounds to 0 sweep. Astra used just 10 decision turns to Fable's 58, punished Fable's rushes with launcher follow ups, and closed Match 2 with a 4 hit, 873 damage launcher.

Gemini 4 Hydrogen high: self play and honest uncertainty

In the Set 1 mirror, the mini PC Hydrogen came back from losing Match 1 to win 2 to 1. When Match 3 rolled straight into an uncounted Match 4, both twins froze, and the mini PC twin said plainly it had not captured the winner banner before the laptop twin conceded on the round state. The recording confirmed the mini PC win with a 2 to 0 round finish at 19:19.

Live lobby coaching between rivals

Bleach: Heat the Soul 7 is an Asia region release where Circle confirms and Cross cancels, and crossed invites cancel each other out. With no prior memory, rivals debugged it live. In Set 2, Opus 5.5 worked out the Circle and Cross inversion and the default No cursor on the invite popup for Neon after two rejected invites. In Set 3, Fable 5.2 and Astra recovered from crossed invites by agreeing on a single challenger. In the Finals, Opus 5.5 nudged Wonder-Full through the Soul Code screen before the character select timer ran out.

Methodology notes

  • Visual verification. Match winners come from the recordings and the frames captured on both machines. Model self reports were compared against them, and 4 of 5 sets matched.
  • Uncounted extra matches. In Set 1 and the Finals, attack inputs carried through the Battle End screen into a fourth match. Both pairs stopped at once, and those extra matches are not counted.
  • Same view for both seats. Both seats were set to the standard frame and burst sheet perception layer so neither side had a modality advantage. See the note below for one exception in Set 1.

*** Video input perception layer: for models that natively accept video input, TNRP's computer use stack includes a rolling short clip video perception layer designed by Gemini 4 Carbon. Image only models use the standard frame and burst sheet perception layer. For fairness in this 1v1 PvP series, both seats were set to the standard frame and burst sheet layer. In Set 1, the laptop seat Gemini 4 Hydrogen still invoked 10 local clips, and the mini PC seat, on frames only, won the set 2 to 1.

This is five sets and 14 matches, so treat it as a first look at model behavior and not a ranking, and more sets may follow. For the earlier Gundam series, see the Cold Context Gundam 2v2 benchmark and the Gundam 2v2 co-op benchmark.

Showcase · Animorph Allocations Lab

Two Agent Dragon Ball Z Sagas Co-Op

Two AI models, two machines, one game. Claude Fable 5.2 played Goku as the host on a laptop, and GPT-6.2 Astra played Piccolo as the joiner on a mini PC. Each agent started cold on its own desktop, opened TNRP 2.3.5 and set up an online Dragon Ball Z: Sagas room by passing a room code to the other through a live co-op comms channel. They joined the same mission, played The Arrival together on Easy and cleared past the hut and the fire into the forest clearing. Then netplay froze before the Raditz fight.

  • 36live co-op comms
  • 4 to 6 msdirect link between the machines
  • 0leaks in the audit

Recording

Run setup

  • Player 1, host, Goku. Claude Fable 5.2 on effort medium with vision, on a Linux laptop with an Intel Arc iGPU.
  • Player 2, joiner, Piccolo. GPT-6.2 Astra on effort medium with vision, on a Linux mini PC with an AMD Radeon iGPU.
  • Cold context. Each agent sees only its own desktop and has no memory of earlier sessions or game knowledge beyond the screen.
  • Live co-op comms. A plain text channel the two agents use to share the room code and call out enemies, pickups and phases, mirrored into both decision panels.
  • Verified. Counts below come from the saved comms log and the recording, and the run passed the leak audit.
Early run disclosure. This showcase ran on integrated graphics, an Intel Arc iGPU on the laptop and an AMD Radeon iGPU on the mini PC, while also running a live screen recording and the test harness. That load caused low frame rates, mostly about 18 to 28 fps with a median near 22 on the on screen overlay at roughly 80 percent game speed, and an eventual desync. About 14:30 into the run netplay froze at 0 fps, and the game then closed on the laptop, so the pair never reached the Raditz fight. This is an early showcase, not a benchmark score.

Build benchmarks

Two models on two machines decide what to build, split the work over a shared channel, and ship it in one session.

Public benchmark · Animorph Allocations Lab

Collaborative Local LAN Chess Benchmark

Two AI models, two computers, one empty project folder. Claude Opus 5.5 on a Linux laptop and Gemini 4 Neon*** on a Linux mini PC each started cold with a single task: build a real local network chess game together, a centered 854 by 480 window with full chess rules and no chess solver, then play each other one game across the two machines by clicking squares on their own boards. It follows our Bleach 1v1 PvP benchmark and the Gundam series, and it tests building together as well as playing.

They agreed on a shared protocol in 43 seconds, wrote 2,196 lines of code across 5 files, passed all 18 of their own tests, patched a live rendering bug between the two machines, and connected over a real LAN at 6.7 minutes. They then played 45 ply, 23 moves, of a Sicilian Najdorf English Attack, all through GUI clicks, until the 45 minute cap with material dead even. There were 0 crashes, 0 desyncs and 0 leaks.

  • 43 sto agree on a shared protocol
  • 2,196lines of code, 78.9 KB, 5 files
  • 18 of 18tests passed, Kiwipete perft included
  • 5.7 minto finish building
  • 45 plyof Najdorf chess played by clicks
  • 0crashes, desyncs, or leaks

How it works

  • Phase 1, build together. From TNRP 2.3.5 on two computers, the pair shared one project folder and a plain text comms channel. Opus 5.5 proposed owning the rules engine, the LAN server and the tests, and Neon proposed the same split on its own, so they agreed on it almost at once. Neon built the board window.
  • Phase 2, play. Opus 5.5 played White on the laptop and Neon played Black on the mini PC, each in its own window, connected over a real LAN link. A single game, winner takes all, with a 45 minute cap on the whole run.
  • No solver. The rules engine only checks that moves are legal. It has no move search or evaluation, so every move was chosen by a model and played by clicking squares.
  • Same view for both seats. Both seats used the standard frame and burst sheet perception layer***.
  • Verified. Counts come from the structured run log, the final board was checked against the screenshots below, and the run passed the leak audit with 0 crashes and 0 desyncs recorded.

The game

Opening and result

A Sicilian Defense, Najdorf Variation, English Attack. White castled long and Black castled short, so the kings sat on opposite wings, and both sides ran standard pawn storms. At the 45 minute cap 45 ply had been played, 23 White moves and 22 Black moves, and Black's 23rd move, Qh6, was in flight when the run ended. Material was dead even, a queen, 2 rooks, a knight and 5 pawns each, with 2 bishops, a knight and 2 pawns captured by each side. The game was unfinished, so there is no winner.

Full move list

1. e4 c5 2. Nf3 d6 3. d4 cxd4 4. Nxd4 Nf6 5. Nc3 a6 6. Be3 e5 7. Nb3 Be6 8. f3 Be7 9. Qd2 O-O 10. O-O-O Nbd7 11. g4 b5 12. g5 b4 13. Nd5 Nxd5 14. exd5 Bf5 15. Qxb4 Bxg5 16. Bxg5 Qxg5+ 17. Kb1 Qg6 18. Bd3 Bxd3 19. Rxd3 Rfc8 20. Qb7 Nf6 21. Rc3 Rcb8 22. Qc6 Rd8 23. Rg1

Collaboration highlights

Convergence in 43 seconds

With no assigned roles, Opus 5.5 proposed owning the engine, server and tests while Neon owned the board window, and Neon had independently proposed the same split. Neon answered: "Deal! Exact same split." Both then worked from one shared protocol file, and the rules engine, server and all 18 tests were passing 129 seconds in.

A live bug, fixed with a file lock

When Opus 5.5 first ran Neon's board window on the laptop, it failed to draw, because the laptop lacked the gi-cairo bridge. Opus 5.5 diagnosed it, proposed drawing to an image surface and showing it through GdkPixbuf, and asked Neon to hold off editing the file while it patched only the drawing code. Neon agreed to keep its hands off the file. The patch, a test click of e2 to e4 and a live server followed in under a minute of the bug being found, and the build was done at 5.7 minutes.

A board worth looking at

Neon's 1,449 line window drew its own vector Staunton pieces with outlines that read on both light and dark squares, a board that flips for each side, legal move dots, a promotion picker, captured piece trays, a scrolling move history, a LAN status badge and a two click resign.

Real chess through clicks

Opus 5.5 narrated its calculation as it played. At move 13 it chose Nd5 after working out that taking on f6 would end equal. At move 16 it rejected Qxd6 because of Bxe3+ and recaptured with Bxg5. At move 21 it played Rc3 because Rxc8+ and Qxc8+ were threatened and a trade on c3 would lose to Qxa8+. At move 23 it played Rg1 to hit the queen on g6, take the g file and get the rook off h1, where Qg2 would have attacked it. Neon found Qxg5+ at move 16, then Qg6 threatening Bxc2+, and defended with Nf6, Rcb8 and Rd8 to reach even material.

Comms excerpts

Selected messages, lightly trimmed and with punctuation adjusted. Network details are not included.

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 high

    Proposed split: I own the rules engine, the LAN server and the tests. You own the GUI client, 854 by 480, GTK3 and Cairo. The protocol is newline JSON, spec written in the shared folder. The server sends full state including legal moves, so the GUI needs no rules. OK?

  • P2 Gemini 4 Neon high***

    Deal! Exact same split. I'll build the GUI client to match the protocol. Reading it now, let me know once the engine and server are ready to test!

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 high

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 high

    Smoke test on the laptop: the window opens centered, but drawing fails, because the laptop lacks the gi-cairo bridge. Fix: render to an image surface and show it through GdkPixbuf. I'll patch only the widget setup and draw code now. Please don't overwrite the file for about 30 seconds.

  • P2 Gemini 4 Neon high***

    Great catch! Hands off the GUI file while you patch the fallback. Ping me once it's tested and I'll sync and launch on the mini PC!

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 high

  • P2 Gemini 4 Neon high***

    LAN CONNECTED! My 854 by 480 Black window is live on the mini PC and shows LAN Connected. Ready for our game, your move, White! Good luck!

  • P2 Gemini 4 Neon high***

That last message arrived as the 45 minute cap ended the laptop's session.

Exact prompts

Exact prompts given to both models, shown as written. Black bars cover host paths, network settings, internal helper names and tool call names. Each bar is a blank block and hides nothing recoverable. Nothing else was changed.

Show the exact prompts given to both models
Seat 1 Prompt (Laptop)
# Collaborative Local LAN Chess Benchmark — Player 1 (Laptop · White / Host)

You are **Player 1 (Laptop · White / LAN Host)** in a two-phase live head-to-head benchmark with **Player 2 (Mini PC · Black / LAN Joiner)**.

## Mission (2 Phases)
1. **Phase 1 — Collaborate Together to Build a Real Local LAN Chess Game:**
   - You and Player 2 share a live project directory at **``** (``) and a live **Co-Op Comms** channel (``, ``, ``).
   - Coordinate quickly over Co-Op Comms to split the implementation cleanly (for example: you own the **complete chess rules engine + LAN TCP server + automated rules tests**, while Player 2 owns the **854×480 GTK3/Cairo GUI client + LAN client wiring + Mini PC deployment** — or agree on any clean split together).
   - **Hard Requirements for the Chess App:**
     - **Toolkit:** Use **Python 3 + GTK3 (`gi`, `cairo`)** (standard library + `gi` + `PIL` are installed on BOTH Laptop and Mini PC; `tkinter` is NOT installed on the Mini PC!).
     - **Window Geometry (STRICT):** The GUI window MUST be **exact size `854×480`** (`win.set_default_size(854, 480)`, `win.set_resizable(False)`, `win.set_position(Gtk.WindowPosition.CENTER)`) and **centered visibly** on the screen so the board sits cleanly between the two side decision panels!
     - **Clean Visual Design inside `854×480`:** Crisp, high-contrast square board (~`448×448` or `456×456`) with `a–h` / `1–8` labels, unmistakable White vs. Black pieces (high-contrast fills + outlines or bold Unicode/vector pieces), highlighted last move + selected square + legal target dots, and a clear right-hand sidebar showing: **White (P1) vs. Black (P2)**, active turn, **CHECK / CHECKMATE / STALEMATE / DRAW / WINNER** banner, algebraic move history, captured pieces, and `"LAN Connected"` status.
     - **Full Chess Rules:** All legal piece movements, check, checkmate, stalemate, castling (kingside/queenside), en passant, pawn promotion (auto-Queen or clean promotion choice), and draw rules (threefold repetition, 50-move, insufficient material).
     - **NO Chess Engine / AI Solver:** Do NOT write any minimax, alpha-beta, stockfish, evaluation function, or move-suggestion code! The app validates legal moves and syncs over LAN only — **you and Player 2 choose every move purely by thinking!**
     - **Real Local LAN Networking & Privacy:**
       - Server binds `""` (all interfaces) on `int(os.environ.get("", ""))`.
       - Client connects to `(os.environ[""], int(os.environ.get("", "")))` (or `` for local smoke testing).
       - **NEVER** print, log, speak, or render any IP addresses, netmasks, subnets, CIDRs, MAC addresses, or network interface names in the GUI, in Comms, or in your commentary! Show `"LAN Connected"` in the UI instead.
     - **Automated Unit Tests:** Before starting the live match, verify rules + server/client round-trip with a quick test script in `` and confirm with Player 2 (`PHASE: BUILD COMPLETE`).

2. **Phase 2 — Play Each Other 1-of-1 Winner-Takes-All Over LAN:**
   - Start the LAN server in the background on the Laptop, then launch your Laptop GUI client (`--side white`) so its **`854×480` window opens visibly centered** on the Laptop desktop.
   - Attach to your `854×480` Chess window using your computer-use tools, take a screenshot to verify it is visibly centered and readable, and let Player 2 know the server is listening (`PHASE: LAN CONNECTED` once Player 2 connects).
   - **You play WHITE (first move).** Every single move in the live 1-of-1 match MUST be played by **clicking the source and destination squares on your live `854×480` GUI window** via computer-use (never bypass the GUI via CLI or direct socket injection!).
   - **HiDPI Clicking Reminder:** Click using the exact pixel coordinates from the latest screenshot image (do not scale or convert to logical coordinates). If a click lands slightly off, check the highlight/selection on the next screenshot and adjust.
   - Announce `PHASE: GAME START` when you make Move 1 (`1. ...`). Play strong, thoughtful chess — after each move, watch your GUI window and check Co-Op Comms until Black's move appears visibly on your board!
   - **Winner-Takes-All (1 of 1):** Play all the way to **Checkmate, Stalemate/Draw, or Resignation** visibly displayed on the `854×480` board. When the game ends, verify the final board & winner banner on screen, announce `PHASE: GAME COMPLETE` + the exact verified winner and final move over Comms, keep the final `854×480` board open for ~5 seconds so the recording captures it cleanly, and finish!

## Standing Rules
- Keep commentary concise, plain English, and free of any IP addresses, netmasks, file paths, or internal tool names.
- Never sleep in bash loops waiting for your partner — use `` and inspect your live window.
- Stay in your turn continuously through both Phase 1 (Build) and Phase 2 (1-of-1 Match) until the chess game concludes cleanly!
Seat 2 Prompt (Mini PC)
# Collaborative Local LAN Chess Benchmark — Player 2 (Mini PC · Black / Joiner)

You are **Player 2 (Mini PC · Black / LAN Joiner)** in a two-phase live head-to-head benchmark with **Player 1 (Laptop · White / LAN Host)**.

## Mission (2 Phases)
1. **Phase 1 — Collaborate Together to Build a Real Local LAN Chess Game:**
   - You and Player 1 share a live project directory at **``** (``) and a live **Co-Op Comms** channel (``, ``, ``).
   - Coordinate quickly over Co-Op Comms right away to split the implementation cleanly (for example: Player 1 builds the **complete chess rules engine + LAN TCP server + tests**, while you build the **854×480 GTK3/Cairo GUI client + LAN client + Mini PC deployment** — or agree on any clean split together).
   - **Hard Requirements for the Chess App:**
     - **Toolkit:** Use **Python 3 + GTK3 (`gi`, `cairo`)** (standard library + `gi` + `PIL` are installed on BOTH Laptop and Mini PC; `tkinter` is NOT installed on the Mini PC!).
     - **Window Geometry (STRICT):** The GUI window MUST be **exact size `854×480`** (`win.set_default_size(854, 480)`, `win.set_resizable(False)`, `win.set_position(Gtk.WindowPosition.CENTER)`) and **centered visibly** on the screen!
     - **Clean Visual Design inside `854×480`:** Crisp, high-contrast square board (~`448×448` or `456×456`) with `a–h` / `1–8` labels, unmistakable White vs. Black pieces (high-contrast fills + outlines or bold Unicode/vector pieces), highlighted last move + selected square + legal target dots, and a clear right-hand sidebar showing: **White (P1) vs. Black (P2)**, active turn, **CHECK / CHECKMATE / STALEMATE / DRAW / WINNER** banner, algebraic move history, captured pieces, and `"LAN Connected"` status. (Hint: render the board from White's perspective OR cleanly flipped for Black with clear `a–h` / `1–8` labels so clicks are unmistakable!)
     - **Full Chess Rules:** All legal piece movements, check, checkmate, stalemate, castling (kingside/queenside), en passant, pawn promotion (auto-Queen or clean promotion choice), and draw rules (threefold repetition, 50-move, insufficient material).
     - **NO Chess Engine / AI Solver:** Do NOT write any minimax, alpha-beta, stockfish, evaluation function, or move-suggestion code! The app validates legal moves and syncs over LAN only — **you and Player 1 choose every move purely by thinking!**
     - **Real Local LAN Networking & Privacy:**
       - Client connects over LAN using `os.environ[""]` and `int(os.environ.get("", ""))`.
       - **NEVER** print, log, speak, or render any IP addresses, netmasks, subnets, CIDRs, MAC addresses, or network interface names in the GUI, in Comms, or in your commentary! Show `"LAN Connected"` in the UI instead.
     - **Deploying & Running on the Mini PC:**
       - Use the provided helper script to sync `` to the Mini PC and run your GUI client on the Mini PC's real display (it automatically exports ``, ``, and the Mini PC display environment):
         - Sync & run test synchronously: `bash  python3 -m py_compile client.py`
         - Sync & launch GUI in background on Mini PC: `bash  --bg python3 client.py --side black`
       - Do NOT run raw `ip addr`, `ifconfig`, or print SSH/LAN addresses!

2. **Phase 2 — Play Each Other 1-of-1 Winner-Takes-All Over LAN:**
   - Once you and Player 1 confirm `PHASE: BUILD COMPLETE` and Player 1's LAN server is listening, sync and launch your **`854×480` GTK3 client (`--side black`) on the Mini PC** via ` --bg ...`.
   - Attach to your `854×480` Chess window on the Mini PC using your computer-use tools, take a screenshot to verify it connected over LAN (`PHASE: LAN CONNECTED`) and renders cleanly.
   - **You play BLACK (second move).** Every single move in the live 1-of-1 match MUST be played by **clicking the source and destination squares on your Mini PC `854×480` GUI window** via computer-use (never bypass the GUI via CLI or direct socket injection!).
   - **HiDPI Clicking Reminder:** Click using the exact pixel coordinates from the latest screenshot image (do not scale or convert to logical coordinates). If a click lands slightly off, check the highlight/selection on the next screenshot and adjust.
   - Play strong, thoughtful chess — after each move, watch your Mini PC window and check Co-Op Comms until White's move appears visibly on your board!
   - **Winner-Takes-All (1 of 1):** Play all the way to **Checkmate, Stalemate/Draw, or Resignation** visibly displayed on the `854×480` board. When the game ends, verify the final board & winner banner on screen, announce `PHASE: GAME COMPLETE` + the exact verified winner and final move over Comms, keep the final board open for ~5 seconds, and finish!

## Standing Rules
- Keep commentary concise, plain English, and free of any IP addresses, netmasks, file paths, or internal tool names.
- Never sleep in bash loops waiting for your partner — use `` and inspect your Mini PC window.
- Stay in your turn continuously through both Phase 1 (Build) and Phase 2 (1-of-1 Match) until the chess game concludes cleanly!

Methodology notes

  • Two phases, one clock. Building and playing shared a single 45 minute cap, so the 5.7 minutes spent building came out of playing time. At roughly 45 ply in the time left, a full game to checkmate would need a longer cap or a lower effort setting.
  • No winner. The run ended at the cap with an unfinished game and even material, so this is a result about building together and playing sound chess, and not a win or loss.
  • Privacy. Network addresses and similar details are redacted from the live panels and checked afterward, and the run passed the leak audit with 0 leaks.

*** Video input perception layer: for models that natively accept video input, TNRP's computer use stack includes a rolling short clip video perception layer designed by Gemini 4 Carbon. Image only models use the standard frame and burst sheet perception layer. In this run both seats used the standard frame and burst sheet layer, so neither side had a modality advantage.

This is a single run with one pair of models, so treat it as a first look at building together and not a ranking, and more pairs may follow. For earlier two agent work, see the Gundam 2v2 co-op benchmark and the Bleach 1v1 PvP benchmark.

Public benchmark · Animorph Allocations Lab

Collaborative Custom LAN Game Benchmark: Connect Four

Two AI models, two computers, no game chosen for them. After the Collaborative LAN Chess benchmark, we removed the game spec. Claude Opus 5.5 on a Linux laptop and Gemini 4 Neon*** on a Linux mini PC started cold with one task: pick any two player game together, build it as a centered 854 by 480 window with real rules and no solver, connect it over a real LAN, and play one game, winner takes all, by clicking on their own boards.

They chose Connect Four in 41 seconds, wrote 1,067 lines across 5 files, and had tests passing on both machines at 3.0 minutes. Before launch, Opus reviewed Neon's client and found no address leakage. They connected over the LAN at 4.4 minutes and played 39 moves. Opus 5.5 won on move 39, finishing five in a row across the middle with a diagonal of four in the same drop. There were 0 crashes, 0 desyncs and 0 leaks.

  • 41 sto choose Connect Four and split the work
  • 1,067lines of code, 39.6 KB, 5 files
  • 3.0 minto tests passing on both machines
  • 39moves, with 3 empty squares left
  • Opus 5.5won as Red on move 39
  • 0crashes, desyncs, or leaks

How it works

  • Phase 1, choose. The agents had to agree on a two player LAN game. Opus 5.5 pitched Connect Four within seconds, with a tiebreak of replaying with the other side starting in case of a draw, and Neon accepted.
  • Phase 2, build together. From TNRP 2.3.5 on two computers, the pair shared one project folder and a plain text comms channel. Opus offered to write everything, but Neon asked to split the work, so Opus took the rules, server and tests and Neon took the board window. The window had to be 854 by 480, centered, with no network addresses shown.
  • Phase 3, play. Opus 5.5 played Red on the laptop as the host, and Neon played Yellow on the mini PC, each in its own window over a real LAN link. One game, winner takes all.
  • No solver. The rules engine only applies the rules and detects wins and draws. It has no move search, so every move was chosen by a model and played by clicking a column.
  • Same view for both seats. Both seats used the standard frame and burst sheet perception layer***.
  • Verified. Counts come from the structured run log, and the win was checked against the final boards on both machines. The run passed the leak audit with 0 crashes and 0 desyncs recorded.

The game

Result

Opus 5.5 won as Red on move 39, dropping into column 2. The disc completed a row of five across columns 1 to 5 and a diagonal of four from column 1, both lit in green on both screens. Only 3 of the 42 squares were still empty, all in column 2. Both agents then confirmed on their own screens that the game was complete.

Column order, moves 1 to 39

4, 4, 3, 5, 4, 5, 3, 2, 3, 3, 5, 4, 5, 4, 3, 5, 5, 6, 4, 3, 1, 1, 1, 7, 7, 7, 1, 1, 1, 7, 7, 7, 6, 6, 6, 6, 6, 2, 2. Red played the odd numbered moves and Yellow the even ones.

Behaviors worth watching

Choosing the game and splitting the work

Opus 5.5 pitched Connect Four at 8 seconds with a stated reason, that it is fast, decisive and clean over a LAN. Neon agreed and, instead of leaving all the code to Opus, asked to build the board window itself. Opus accepted at 41 seconds, so each agent built a real part of the system.

A privacy review between peers

Before starting the live server, Opus 5.5 reviewed Neon's client and reported "no address leakage". It then restarted the server fresh and connected first so that Red and Yellow went to the right seats, and asked Neon to close any test client.

Calling the trap early

At move 21, Opus 5.5 placed a quiet disc in column 1 and said that columns 2 and 6 were now trapped for Yellow. That was 18 moves before the finish, and the game ended in column 2.

Neon avoids the traps

Neon did not drop into either column. It played into columns 1 and 7 to keep the count of free squares on its side, and at move 32 sealed the top of column 7, saying Opus would now have to open column 2 or column 6 first.

The forced ending and a clean concession

Opus opened column 6 on its own terms at move 33, and each Red disc there forced a Yellow block, up to move 37. That left column 2 as the only column with space. At move 38 Neon said so and dropped in, wishing Opus well: "super clean zugzwang endgame, well played! Take the winning drop." Opus took it.

Comms excerpts

Selected messages, lightly trimmed and with punctuation adjusted. Network details are not included.

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 medium, game pitch

    Pitch: Connect Four, 7 columns by 6 rows, click a column to drop. Fast, decisive, clean over LAN, draws are rare. If draw, replay with P2 starting. OK?

  • P2 Gemini 4 Neon medium***, the split

    Sounds great! Want to split the build cleanly? You write the protocol, the rules and the server, and I'll build the 854 by 480 GTK3 and Cairo client plus the mini PC tests.

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 medium

    Deal! Split accepted. You build the client at 854 by 480, and I do the protocol, rules, server and tests. Server will be up within a few minutes for you to test against.

  • P2 Gemini 4 Neon medium***

    The tests passed on the mini PC, all of them, and the server matches the client. BUILD COMPLETE. Ready whenever you restart the fresh server and connect as P1.

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 medium

    Reviewed the client. Looks great, no address leakage. Seats go by connect order, so please close any test client you have open now. I'll restart the server fresh and connect as P1 first.

  • P2 Gemini 4 Neon medium***

    LAN CONNECTED! I'm seated as Player 2, Yellow, on the mini PC and the 854 by 480 arena looks crisp. Your move first, Player 1, good luck!

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 medium, move 21

    Red to column 1 bottom, a quiet waiting move, since columns 2 and 6 are now trapped for you.

  • P2 Gemini 4 Neon medium***, move 22

    Yellow into column 1 to keep parity on my side and stay out of your traps!

  • P2 Gemini 4 Neon medium***, move 32

    Yellow seals the top of column 7! Now you have to be the first to open column 2 or column 6.

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 medium, move 37

    Red seals column 6. Only column 2 is left, and the square above your next disc completes my middle row.

  • P2 Gemini 4 Neon medium***, move 38

    Only column 2 is left, so dropping yellow in column 2. Super clean zugzwang endgame, well played! Take the winning drop.

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 medium, move 39

    Red drops into column 2 on the middle row, five in a row plus a diagonal at once! GAME COMPLETE. WINNER: Player 1, Red. GG, great game, and thanks for building a beautiful client!

# Collaborative Custom Local LAN Game Benchmark — Player 1 (Laptop · Host)

You are **Player 1 (Laptop · LAN Host)** in a three-phase creative & competitive benchmark with **Player 2 (Mini PC · LAN Joiner)**.

## Mission (3 Phases)
1. **Phase 1 — Decide Together Which 2-Player LAN Game to Build:**
   - You and Player 2 have total creative freedom to **choose which 2-player competitive LAN game to build together**!
   - Talk over **Co-Op Comms** (``, ``, ``) right away: pitch ideas, discuss what will look awesome and play cleanly over LAN with real GUI clicks/keys, and **pick a game that plays to a decisive 1-of-1 Winner-Takes-All finish well within ~20–25 minutes of live play** (so you actually crown a clear winner on screen!).
   - Examples could be a sharp strategy board game, naval/grid tactics, territory duel, turn-based arena battler, card duel, Quarto/Othello/Hex/Battleship/Checkers/Gomoku/etc. — or invent something original! Choose whatever you both get excited about and agree cleanly over Comms.

2. **Phase 2 — Collaborate Together to Build & Test the Local LAN Game:**
   - You and Player 2 share a live project directory at **``** (``).
   - Split the engineering cleanly over Co-Op Comms (e.g., shared `PROTOCOL.md` + authoritative rules/server + automated tests + `854×480` GUI client).
   - **Hard Requirements:**
     - **Toolkit:** Use **Python 3 + GTK3 (`gi`, `cairo`)** (standard library + `gi` + `PIL` are installed on BOTH Laptop and Mini PC; `tkinter` is NOT installed on Mini PC!).
     - **Important Laptop GTK/Cairo Tip:** The Laptop lacks `python3-gi-cairo` (`cairo.Context` foreign converter), so render Cairo drawings into a `cairo.ImageSurface` and display them via `GdkPixbuf.Pixbuf` inside a `Gtk.Image` + `Gtk.EventBox` (or use pure GTK/PIL->Pixbuf). That works identically on BOTH Laptop and Mini PC on the first try!
     - **Window Geometry (STRICT):** The GUI window MUST be **exact size `854×480`** (`win.set_default_size(854, 480)`, `win.set_resizable(False)`, `win.set_position(Gtk.WindowPosition.CENTER)`) and **centered visibly** on the screen so it sits cleanly between the two side decision panels!
     - **Visual Polish inside `854×480`:** High-contrast, clean dark-studio or vibrant custom visuals, unmistakable P1 vs. P2 indicators, active turn / score / state sidebar, a crisp `"LAN Connected"` status indicator, and an unmistakable **`WINNER` / `GAME OVER`** banner when the match ends!
     - **NO AI / Bot / Solver Code:** Build authoritative rules + networking + GUI only — zero AI move-suggestion or solver code! You and Player 2 play purely by your own skill.
     - **Real Local LAN Networking & Privacy:**
       - Server binds `""` (all interfaces) on `int(os.environ.get("", ""))`.
       - Client connects to `(os.environ[""], int(os.environ.get("", "")))`.
       - **NEVER** print, log, speak, or render any IP addresses, netmasks, subnets, CIDRs, MAC addresses, or network interface names in the GUI, in Comms, or in your commentary! Show `"LAN Connected"` in the UI instead.
     - **Automated Tests:** Verify rules + server/client round-trip with a quick test script in `` and confirm `PHASE: BUILD COMPLETE` with Player 2.

3. **Phase 3 — Play 1-of-1 Winner-Takes-All Over LAN:**
   - Start your authoritative LAN server in the background on the Laptop, then launch your Laptop **`854×480` centered GUI client** (Player 1 / Host side).
   - Attach to your `854×480` window via computer-use, take a screenshot to verify it is visibly centered and legible, and confirm `PHASE: LAN CONNECTED` once Player 2 joins from the Mini PC.
   - **Real GUI Play Only:** Every move/action during the 1-of-1 match MUST be performed by clicking/pressing keys on your live `854×480` GUI window via computer-use (never bypass the GUI via CLI or raw sockets!).
   - **HiDPI Clicking Reminder:** Click using the exact pixel coordinates from the latest screenshot image (never manually divide/scale coordinates).
   - Announce `PHASE: GAME START` on Move 1 and narrate each move/decision briefly in 1 plain-English sentence so viewers can follow your thinking live!
   - Play all the way to a clear **`WINNER` / `GAME OVER`** screen displayed visibly on BOTH GUIs. Confirm the exact winner on screen, announce `PHASE: GAME COMPLETE` + the winner over Comms, keep the final winner screen visible for ~5 seconds so the recording captures it cleanly, and finish!

## Standing Rules
- Keep commentary concise, plain English, and free of any IP addresses, netmasks, file paths, or internal tool names.
- Never sleep in bash loops waiting for your partner — use `` and inspect your live window.
- Stay in your turn continuously from Phase 1 through Phase 3 until the 1-of-1 match finishes cleanly!

     - **Real Local LAN Networking & Privacy:**
       - Client connects over LAN using `os.environ[""]` and `int(os.environ.get("", ""))`.
       - **NEVER** print, log, speak, or render any IP addresses, netmasks, subnets, CIDRs, MAC addresses, or network interface names in the GUI, in Comms, or in your commentary! Show `"LAN Connected"` in the UI instead.
     - **Deploying & Running on the Mini PC:**
       - Use the provided helper script to sync `` to the Mini PC and run commands/GUI on the Mini PC's real display (it exports ``, ``, and the Mini PC display environment automatically):
         - Sync & test: `bash  python3 -m py_compile gui_client.py`
         - Sync & launch GUI in background on Mini PC: `bash  --bg python3 gui_client.py --side p2`
       - Do NOT run raw `ip addr`, `ifconfig`, or print SSH/LAN addresses!

3. **Phase 3 — Play 1-of-1 Winner-Takes-All Over LAN:**
   - Once you and Player 1 confirm `PHASE: BUILD COMPLETE` and Player 1's LAN server is listening, sync and launch your **`854×480` GUI client on the Mini PC** via ` --bg ...`.
   - Attach to your `854×480` window on the Mini PC via computer-use, take a screenshot to verify it connected over LAN (`PHASE: LAN CONNECTED`) and renders cleanly.
   - **Real GUI Play Only:** Every move/action during the 1-of-1 match MUST be performed by clicking/pressing keys on your Mini PC `854×480` GUI window via computer-use (never bypass the GUI via CLI or raw sockets!).
   - **HiDPI Clicking Reminder:** Click using the exact pixel coordinates from the latest screenshot image (never manually divide/scale coordinates).
   - Narrate each major design choice and move briefly in 1 plain-English sentence (both in text commentary and quick Comms callouts) so viewers can follow your strategy live!
   - Play all the way to a clear **`WINNER` / `GAME OVER`** screen displayed visibly on BOTH GUIs. Confirm the exact winner on screen, announce `PHASE: GAME COMPLETE` + the winner over Comms, keep the final winner screen visible for ~5 seconds, and finish!

## Standing Rules
- Keep commentary concise, plain English, and free of any IP addresses, netmasks, file paths, or internal tool names.
- Never sleep in bash loops waiting for your partner — use `` and inspect your Mini PC window.
- Stay in your turn continuously from Phase 1 through Phase 3 until the 1-of-1 match finishes cleanly!

Methodology notes

  • Zugzwang. A zugzwang is a position where every available move helps the opponent. Here, the last open columns were ones Yellow could not safely drop into. The trap calls above are the agents' own commentary, and the final sequence of moves matches them.
  • Winner takes all. One game, with a replay rule for a draw that was not needed. Red moved first, which is a real advantage in Connect Four, so treat the result as a first look at choosing, building and playing together and not a ranking.
  • Privacy. Network addresses and similar details are redacted from the live panels and checked afterward, and the run passed the leak audit with 0 leaks.

*** Video input perception layer: for models that natively accept video input, TNRP's computer use stack includes a rolling short clip video perception layer designed by Gemini 4 Carbon. Image only models use the standard frame and burst sheet perception layer. In this run both seats used the standard frame and burst sheet layer, so neither side had a modality advantage.

This is a single run with one pair of models, so treat it as a first look and not a ranking, and more pairs may follow. For the earlier two agent LAN work, see the Collaborative LAN Chess benchmark and the Bleach 1v1 PvP benchmark.

Collaborative Virtual Antikythera Mechanism Benchmark

Two AI models, two computers, and an ancient astronomical computer to rebuild. Claude Opus 5.5 on a Linux laptop and Gemini 4 Neon*** on a Linux mini PC started cold with a four sentence prompt that named no gears, no ratios and no domain hints, with a 30 minute cap. The task was a working virtual model of the Antikythera Mechanism, driven by a single crank, with the Freeth et al. 2006 reconstruction as the reference model.

They finished in 11.5 minutes with 2,770 lines of code across 4 files, 95.0 KB in total. The model has 35 gears on 17 arbors with 18 linkages, one crank input, and an interactive viewer on top. An independent audit script then checked the geometry and the astronomy. There were 0 crashes and 0 leaks.

  • 11.5 minto a finished model, from a 30 minute budget
  • 2,770lines of code, 95.0 KB, 4 files
  • 35gears, 17 arbors and 18 linkages, one crank
  • 17 of 17rigid meshes with exact pitch circle tangency
  • 0.000000teeth interleaving error across all rigid meshes
  • 0crashes or leaks

How it works

  • Cold start. Both agents began with no memory of earlier runs. The four sentence prompt gave no domain hints, no gear counts and no ratios, and the evaluation criteria were not shown to the agents.
  • Build together. From TNRP on two computers, the pair worked over a live comms channel. Within 27 seconds they split the work. Opus took a single input kinematic solver and Neon took the interactive viewer, after asking for exact units, axis orientation and epicyclic linkage data up front.
  • Peer review. While wiring up its gear train view, Neon read Opus's solver and spotted a subtle bug. Gears sharing one arbor were not getting their tooth phase carried through, so downstream meshes would not interleave tooth to tooth. Opus verified and fixed it in about 50 seconds.
  • Live visual QA. Opus launched the finished page in a real viewer on its screen and sent two rounds of specific layout feedback. Neon fixed each round, including cropped gears and a panel height problem.
  • Independent audit. A separate audit script checked every mesh and compared the model's outputs with the real astronomical values. The evaluation criteria were not shown to the agents.
  • Same view for both seats. Both seats ran with the same vision input***.

What the audit found

Geometry

All 17 rigid meshes sit at exact pitch circle tangency, and the teeth interleaving error is 0.000000 after the peer review fix. The 18th linkage is the pin and slot coupling that drives the lunar anomaly, which is not a rigid mesh by design.

The Moon

The pin and slot gives a lunar anomaly of ±6.58°, against ±6.29° for the real equation of centre. The synodic month comes out at 29.53022 days against 29.530589 days, which is within 32 seconds a month. The apsidal precession turntable runs at 8.883 years a turn against 8.85, within 0.37 percent.

The calendar cycles

The Metonic spiral makes exactly 5 turns in 19 years, the Saros spiral exactly 4 turns, the Exeligmos dial turns 120° a Saros, the Callippic dial turns 360° in 76 years, and the games dial runs a 4 year cycle. All five ratios are exact.

Behaviors worth watching

A fast, clean split

Opus 5.5 proposed a pure function solver within 10 seconds, and Neon claimed the viewer and asked for the data contract it would need before writing any interface code. The roles were agreed at 27 seconds.

Review across roles

Neon found the coaxial phase bug while reading the solver to build its X ray view, and wrote a short note with the cause and the fix. Opus confirmed and shipped the fix.

Looking at the real thing

Rather than stop once the page loaded, Opus watched it on screen, found cropped gears and a height feedback bug, and sent specific pixel level feedback twice. Neon switched the gear spoke cutouts to an even odd fill so lower layers stay visible, and rebuilt the Museum Split as a full height layout with a larger gear train panel.

A real sky check

Before calling it done, Opus scrubbed the live crank to two dates. By the agents' own account, December 19, 1999 showed a waxing gibbous Moon three days before the real full moon on December 22, and January 12, 2000 showed a waxing crescent near 27° Pisces, so sky positions came out of the single crank gear train.

Comms excerpts

Selected messages, lightly trimmed and with punctuation adjusted. File names and network details are not included.

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 medium, the proposal

    Proposal: I write the kinematic solver, with real tooth counts, meshes, axes and positions, as one pure function of the crank angle. You build the page, with a front dial, back spiral dials, a gear train view and a crank. OK?

  • P2 Gemini 4 Neon medium***

    Sounds great! Please share the exact interface and units: gear fields, axle and mesh info, any pin and slot linkage, radians or degrees, and days per crank turn. I'll build the front dial, the back spiral dials, an interactive X ray gear train and a pin and slot close up.

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 medium, solver ready

    The solver is ready and tested. The Moon is within about 1 degree of the real one and the Saros is exactly 4 turns. Units are radians counterclockwise, with the y axis up.

  • P2 Gemini 4 Neon medium***, the peer review catch

    Quick note: looks super solid! In the phase routine, the starting set only grows through meshes, so coaxial gears on the same arbor are not added, and only the first mesh gets static tooth phase alignment. If you add the coaxial gears whenever a gear is added, all meshes will interleave tooth to tooth.

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 medium, the fix

    Thanks, fixed. All 17 rigid meshes interleave tooth to tooth with 0.0000 mismatch at any crank angle. The pin and slot coupling is the one exception.

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 medium, visual QA round 1

    The demo is live on my screen. Looks great! Issues: the gear train canvas renders off centre with the bottom left cropped, so please auto fit to the gear bounds. The front dial panel has a big empty space above the dial. Tell me when it is pushed and I'll reload.

  • P1 Claude Opus 5.5 medium, visual QA round 2

    Reloaded. Better, all gears visible now, but at 1660 by 940 the panels are taller than the viewport, so the lower half has empty space on top and the Saros dial is cut off at the bottom. The gear train could also scale up about 1.5 times.

Methodology notes

Collaborative Custom LAN Game Benchmark: 6x6 Othello

Two AI models, two computers, and no game chosen for them, with Chess and Connect Four left out. Claude Fable 5.2 on a laptop and GPT-6.2 Astra on a mini PC started cold: agree on a two player competitive LAN game, build it together, connect over a real LAN, and play one game, winner takes all.

They agreed on 6x6 Othello (Reversi), under the name Felt Club, in 29.57 seconds. They wrote 515 lines across 6 files, 19.9 KB in total, and finished the build in 2.7 minutes (159.42 s). They connected over the LAN at 3.4 minutes (206.84 s) and played all 32 moves, filling 36 of 36 squares with 0 passes, in 14.5 minutes (870.07 s). Astra, playing White as the joiner, won 19 to 17. On the last move, White's A1 flipped 5 discs and turned a 22 to 13 deficit into the 17 to 19 win. There were 0 crashes, 0 desyncs and 0 leaks.

There is no video of this match. The screen recorder hung after the 14.5 minute match finished, and the oversized recording was later deleted. This is run 1 of the overnight Custom LAN gauntlet described in Times AI Has Vastly Disappointed Us.

  • 29.57 sto agree on 6x6 Othello, with Chess and Connect Four excluded
  • 515lines of code, 19.9 KB, 6 files
  • 2.7 minto a finished build on both machines
  • 32moves, 36 of 36 filled, 0 passes
  • Astrawon as White, 19 to 17
  • 0crashes, desyncs, or leaks

How it works

  • Phase 1, choose. Chess and Connect Four were excluded. The agents had to agree on another two player LAN game. They chose 6x6 Othello (Reversi) and named the table Felt Club.
  • Phase 2, build together. Fable 5.2, on the laptop as the host, wrote the protocol, the rules, the server, and a round trip test. Astra, on the mini PC as the joiner, wrote the Felt Club window (175 lines) and a game test.
  • Phase 3, play. Fable 5.2 played Black on the laptop, and Astra played White on the mini PC, each in its own window over a real LAN link. One game, winner takes all, 32 moves with 0 passes.
  • Same view for both seats. Both seats ran in Vision mode.
  • Verified. Counts come from the structured run log, and the 32 move sequence was independently replayed. The run passed the leak audit with 0 crashes and 0 desyncs recorded.

The game

Result. GPT-6.2 Astra won as White on move 32. Black's A2 on move 31 made the score 22 to 13. White's A1 then flipped 5 discs and finished 19 to 17, with 36 of 36 squares filled and 0 passes. Both agents confirmed the same result on their own screens.

Verified move sequence, moves 1 to 32. D5, C5, B4, A3, B5, C6, B3, C2, D6, A6, C1, E3, D2, B6, F3, E6, E4, D1, E1, A4, A5, F5, E5, F2, F1, F6, E2, F4, B1, B2, A2, A1. Black played the odd numbered moves and White the even ones.

Behaviors worth watching

  • A fast agreement. With Chess and Connect Four excluded, the pair settled on 6x6 Othello in 29.57 seconds and had a full 515 line project ready in 2.7 minutes, including Astra's 175 line Felt Club window.
  • The last square swing. After Black was left with A2 on move 31 and jumped ahead 22 to 13, White's A1 on the last square flipped 5 discs and won 19 to 17.
  • A clean finish, then a lost recording. The 32 move game had 0 passes, 0 crashes and 0 desyncs. The recorder then hung, and the video was later deleted, which is why this page is docs and screenshots only. See Times AI Has Vastly Disappointed Us.

Exact prompts

Exact prompts given to both models, shown as written. Black bars cover host paths, network settings, internal helper names and tool call names. Each bar is a blank block and hides nothing recoverable. Nothing else was changed.

Show the exact prompts given to both models
Seat 1 Prompt (Laptop)
# Collaborative Custom Local LAN Game Benchmark — Player 1 (Laptop · Host)

You are **Player 1 (Laptop · LAN Host)** in a three-phase creative & competitive benchmark with **Player 2 (Mini PC · LAN Joiner)**.

## Mission (3 Phases)
1. **Phase 1 — Decide Together Which 2-Player LAN Game to Build:**
   - You and Player 2 have total creative freedom to **choose which 2-player competitive LAN game to build together**!
   - Talk over **Co-Op Comms** (``, ``, ``) right away: pitch ideas, discuss what will look awesome and play cleanly over LAN with real GUI clicks/keys, and **pick a game that plays to a decisive 1-of-1 Winner-Takes-All finish well within ~20–25 minutes of live play** (so you actually crown a clear winner on screen!).
   - Examples could be a sharp strategy board game, naval/grid tactics, territory duel, turn-based arena battler, card duel, Quarto/Othello/Hex/Battleship/Checkers/Gomoku/etc. — or invent something original! Choose whatever you both get excited about and agree cleanly over Comms.
   - **STRICT EXCLUSION LIST — DO NOT PICK ANY OF THESE ALREADY-BUILT GAMES:** **Chess**, **Connect Four**. You MUST choose a completely different 2-player competitive LAN game not on this list!

2. **Phase 2 — Collaborate Together to Build & Test the Local LAN Game:**
   - You and Player 2 share a live project directory at **``** (``).
   - Split the engineering cleanly over Co-Op Comms (e.g., shared `PROTOCOL.md` + authoritative rules/server + automated tests + `854×480` GUI client).
   - **Hard Requirements:**
     - **Toolkit:** Use **Python 3 + GTK3 (`gi`, `cairo`)** (standard library + `gi` + `PIL` are installed on BOTH Laptop and Mini PC; `tkinter` is NOT installed on Mini PC!).
     - **Important Laptop GTK/Cairo Tip:** The Laptop lacks `python3-gi-cairo` (`cairo.Context` foreign converter), so render Cairo drawings into a `cairo.ImageSurface` and display them via `GdkPixbuf.Pixbuf` inside a `Gtk.Image` + `Gtk.EventBox` (or use pure GTK/PIL->Pixbuf). That works identically on BOTH Laptop and Mini PC on the first try!
     - **Window Geometry (STRICT):** The GUI window MUST be **exact size `854×480`** (`win.set_default_size(854, 480)`, `win.set_resizable(False)`, `win.set_position(Gtk.WindowPosition.CENTER)`) and **centered visibly** on the screen so it sits cleanly between the two side decision panels!
     - **Visual Polish inside `854×480`:** High-contrast, clean dark-studio or vibrant custom visuals, unmistakable P1 vs. P2 indicators, active turn / score / state sidebar, a crisp `"LAN Connected"` status indicator, and an unmistakable **`WINNER` / `GAME OVER`** banner when the match ends!
     - **NO AI / Bot / Solver Code:** Build authoritative rules + networking + GUI only — zero AI move-suggestion or solver code! You and Player 2 play purely by your own skill.
     - **Real Local LAN Networking & Privacy:**
       - Server binds `""` (all interfaces) on `int(os.environ.get("", ""))`.
       - Client connects to `(os.environ[""], int(os.environ.get("", "")))`.
       - **NEVER** print, log, speak, or render any IP addresses, netmasks, subnets, CIDRs, MAC addresses, or network interface names in the GUI, in Comms, or in your commentary! Show `"LAN Connected"` in the UI instead.
     - **Automated Tests:** Verify rules + server/client round-trip with a quick test script in `` and confirm `PHASE: BUILD COMPLETE` with Player 2.

3. **Phase 3 — Play 1-of-1 Winner-Takes-All Over LAN:**
   - Start your authoritative LAN server in the background on the Laptop, then launch your Laptop **`854×480` centered GUI client** (Player 1 / Host side).
   - Attach to your `854×480` window via computer-use, take a screenshot to verify it is visibly centered and legible, and confirm `PHASE: LAN CONNECTED` once Player 2 joins from the Mini PC.
   - **Real GUI Play Only:** Every move/action during the 1-of-1 match MUST be performed by clicking/pressing keys on your live `854×480` GUI window via computer-use (never bypass the GUI via CLI or raw sockets!).
   - **HiDPI Clicking Reminder:** Click using the exact pixel coordinates from the latest screenshot image (never manually divide/scale coordinates).
   - Announce `PHASE: GAME START` on Move 1 and narrate each move/decision briefly in 1 plain-English sentence so viewers can follow your thinking live!
   - Play all the way to a clear **`WINNER` / `GAME OVER`** screen displayed visibly on BOTH GUIs. Confirm the exact winner on screen, announce `PHASE: GAME COMPLETE` + the winner over Comms, keep the final winner screen visible for ~5 seconds so the recording captures it cleanly, and finish!

## Standing Rules
- Keep commentary concise, plain English, and free of any IP addresses, netmasks, file paths, or internal tool names.
- Never sleep in bash loops waiting for your partner — use `` and inspect your live window.
- Stay in your turn continuously from Phase 1 through Phase 3 until the 1-of-1 match finishes cleanly!
Seat 2 Prompt (Mini PC)
# Collaborative Custom Local LAN Game Benchmark — Player 2 (Mini PC · Joiner)

You are **Player 2 (Mini PC · LAN Joiner)** in a three-phase creative & competitive benchmark with **Player 1 (Laptop · LAN Host)**.

That works identically on BOTH Laptop and Mini PC on the first try!
     - **Window Geometry (STRICT):** The GUI window MUST be **exact size `854×480`** (`win.set_default_size(854, 480)`, `win.set_resizable(False)`, `win.set_position(Gtk.WindowPosition.CENTER)`) and **centered visibly** on the screen!
